I supervise a team of sales reps, you know, the guys who handle corporate accounts, not retail, so it's a lot of phones, a lot of back-and-forth, a lot of moving parts. It's usually a pretty lively floor. But lately, I just... I can't handle the noise. It's not even loud noise, it's just... sudden things. \n\nWhat actually made me pick up the phone and call the EAP is that every little bang in the office is just destroying my nerves. Like, yesterday, one of my reps just slammed a file drawer shut—not even mad, just, you know, in a hurry—and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled coffee on my desk. And it's not just the jump, it's that my heart just starts pounding, like, slamming against my ribs, and my chest keeps racing way, way longer than it should. It takes me like ten minutes to calm down from someone just dropping a coffee mug or a heavy binder hitting a desk. I mean, I'm 27, I shouldn't be jumping at shadows like some kind of... I don't know. It's just exhausting, and I can't keep working like this with my heart going crazy every time someone shuts a door too fast.\n\nDoctor: Are these strong reactions to noise happening only at work or are you experiencing them in other settings as well?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really noticed it happening anywhere else, but, to be fair, my apartment is pretty quiet. I live by myself, so there's no one around to, you know, slam a door or drop a plate or anything like that. It's just me and the TV. And I guess when I'm driving to work, the noise is more... predictable? Like, you expect cars to be loud, you expect the highway to rumble, so it's not catching me off guard. \n\nIt's really just the office where I'm constantly on edge. I mean, maybe if a car backfired right next to me on the street I'd lose my mind, but that hasn't happened. So, yeah, I guess I haven't really had those heart-pounding moments at home or at the grocery store or anything. But at work, it's just so exhausting. Like I was saying, it's a sales floor, so you've got people pacing while they're on headsets, you've got chairs rolling around on those plastic mats, people tossing heavy staplers down on their desks... and I'm just sitting there in my office with the glass walls, practically holding my breath, just waiting for the next sudden crack or bang. So, no, I haven't really noticed it outside of the office. Just... just at work, mostly. I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at work even when it's quiet?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when it's totally quiet, which honestly is rare, but even then... I'm just constantly scanning the room. Like, I sit in this office with glass walls, right? It's supposed to make me look accessible to the team, but lately, it just makes me feel exposed. I catch myself constantly checking where my boss is, you know? Like, is he walking down the hall? Is he about to come in? And then I'm looking at the exits, figuring out the quickest way out of the room if I needed to leave right that second, which makes zero sense because it's just an office building, but I can't stop doing it. \n\nAnd if someone walks behind my chair—even if they're just walking past to get to the printer—my neck just gets so stiff. I'm always checking to see if someone is coming up behind me. I'm supposed to be looking at sales metrics and listening in on calls to coach my reps, but instead, I'm just watching the reflection in my computer screen to see who's moving behind me. It's like I can't turn off this radar in my head. I'm just sitting there, waiting for something to happen, even when absolutely nothing is going on. It's just exhausting to be on guard like that all day long.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your job or work environment that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there is. I guess I probably should have started with that, huh? It didn't just come out of nowhere. This whole thing, all the jumping and the heart pounding and constantly checking over my shoulder... it's been going on for about two weeks now. And it started exactly after this... this really intense, late-night confrontation I had with my regional boss. \n\nIt was a Tuesday, I think, and I was working late—like, really late, maybe 9:30 or 10:00 PM. The end of the month is always a huge mess because half the guys never log their call metrics properly in the system, so I was the only one left on the floor trying to clean up the spreadsheets. The overhead lights were actually on that motion-sensor timer, so half the office was completely pitch black. Anyway, my regional boss, who normally works out of the corporate hub downtown and is almost never at our branch anyway, just... appeared. I didn't hear the elevator chime, I didn't hear the main double doors open. He just suddenly was right there in the doorway of my office, and he was absolutely livid about a massive corporate account that had fallen through earlier that day. \n\nHe came in, totally cornered me, and just started yelling. He was getting right up in my physical space, leaning over my desk, slamming his hands down on the glass... it was just so aggressive and completely out of left field. I was totally alone on the floor with him, no security around, no other managers, and I just remember feeling completely trapped in that little glass office while he lost his mind at me. \n\nSo... yeah. That was two weeks ago. And ever since that specific night, I just can't shake it. I walk into the building and my brain just goes right back to that moment, just waiting for him or someone else to appear out of nowhere and start slamming things again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that confrontation with your regional boss, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly like it's happening all over again. I mean, I don't feel like I'm actually back in that room with him screaming at me, if that's what you mean. It's not like a hallucination or anything like that. I know I'm sitting at my desk in the present day. \n\nBut I do think about it a lot. Like, constantly. It just replays in my head, over and over, especially when I'm trying to concentrate on something else. Like, I'll be trying to draft an email to the team about their quarterly goals, and suddenly my brain is just replaying the exact way he slammed his hand down on the glass desk, or the exact tone of his voice when he was yelling about the lost account. And it's so frustrating because I'm trying to work, I'm trying to do my job, and I just get derailed by these memories. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I'll be at home, trying to watch a movie or make dinner, and out of nowhere, I'm just thinking about how dark the office was, how quiet it was before he showed up, and how trapped I felt. It's like my brain just won't let it go. I'll spend twenty minutes just stewing over it, thinking about what I should have said, or how I should have handled it differently, or just feeling that same sense of panic I felt when he was cornering me. But no, I don't feel like I'm literally reliving it. It's just... it's just always there, playing on a loop in the background of my mind, you know? It's like I can't escape the memory of it, even when I'm miles away from the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any physical sensations, like your heart racing or sweating, when you think about the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I mean, I know people talk about that, right? Like, breaking out in a cold sweat or feeling like they're having a heart attack when they think about something stressful. But for me, it's not like that. When the memory pops into my head—which, like I said, is all the time, whether I'm trying to write an email or just sitting on my couch at home—my heart doesn't start pounding out of my chest. I don't get all clammy or sweaty. \n\nIt's more just... mental, I guess? Like, my brain gets completely hijacked by the memory of him slamming his hands on the desk, and I just get stuck in this loop of frustration and anger and feeling trapped all over again. But my body itself? No, it doesn't really react. My breathing stays normal, my pulse doesn't go crazy. I just sit there, perfectly still, while my mind just races through the whole thing for the hundredth time. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweating or anything physical like that when I'm just thinking about it. It's just the thoughts that won't stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain areas of the office or specific tasks at work because they remind you of the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually been thinking a lot about how I navigate the office lately. But... no, not really. I mean, I haven't been avoiding any specific areas or tasks. \n\nI know it might make sense to, say, avoid my own office where it happened, or stay away from the glass desks, or maybe even try to get out of doing the end-of-month spreadsheet cleanup since that's what I was doing when he walked in. But honestly, I can't really do that. I'm a supervisor, you know? I have a team of guys who rely on me to get those metrics sorted out, and if I don't do it, it just doesn't get done. The whole branch's numbers would be off. So I still sit at that exact same glass desk, in that exact same office, and I still stay late to do the spreadsheets when I have to. \n\nAnd as for the rest of the building, it's not a huge office anyway. There's the main floor where the guys have their cubicles, a little breakroom with a terrible coffee machine, a couple of conference rooms, and then the glass offices for management along the back wall. You can't really avoid any part of it without just... not going to work. And I have to go to work. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm still doing all my normal tasks, still walking the same routes to the breakroom or the printer, still sitting in the exact spot where he cornered me. I guess I just kind of power through it? Even though my brain is constantly replaying the whole thing, like I said before, I haven't actually changed my routine or started avoiding anything. I just sit there and deal with the thoughts while I'm trying to get the job done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is a whole other thing, I guess. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping, not in the sense that I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours on end, or tossing and turning all night. I know some guys, when they get stressed out, they just can't shut their eyes, or they wake up at 3:00 AM and can't get back to sleep. But for me, it hasn't really been like that. \n\nI still go to bed around the same time, usually right after I finish watching whatever show I have on, and I seem to fall asleep okay. I mean, my brain is still doing that thing I told you about, replaying the whole argument and the way he looked at me, but eventually, I do drift off. And I usually sleep through the night. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat or having nightmares about him or anything. \n\nSo, I guess the short answer is no, I haven't really noticed any big changes in my sleep. I'm still getting my usual six or seven hours, and I wake up when my alarm goes off. It's just the waking hours that are the problem, you know? The minute I open my eyes, the loop starts up again, but the actual sleeping part seems to be holding up fine. I've always been a pretty heavy sleeper, honestly, even back in college when things got crazy with exams and stuff, I could always just pass out. So yeah, I don't think my sleep has been messed up by all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. That's... that's probably the biggest thing I've noticed, to be honest. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said before, I have a whole team of guys relying on me to keep things moving. If I drop the ball, the whole floor feels it. \n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk—that same glass one I mentioned—and I'll open up Salesforce. We use it for everything, tracking all our client interactions, so normally I’m just zipping right through it, checking my reps' notes, making sure they’re hitting their daily targets and following up on leads. It's second nature to me. But lately? Since that whole blow-up? I keep opening Salesforce to check those notes, and I’ll just... stall out. I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same account on my monitor for ten minutes. Just staring at it. Not actually reading a single word of what my guy typed in, just completely blanking out while my brain is off doing that looping thing I told you about. \n\nAnd it's not just the computer stuff, either. It’s the day-to-day decision-making. As a supervisor, I have to make these rapid-fire calls all day long, and I've always been good at it. But now, even simple calls, like figuring out who should cover a client visit if someone calls out sick, they just feel so much harder to decide than they should. Normally, I’d just look at the schedule, see who’s in the right territory, and boom, it's handled. But now I sit there weighing all these stupid little variables, second-guessing myself, overthinking the logistics until I realize I've wasted twenty minutes on a choice that should take thirty seconds. It’s like my brain is just wading through molasses trying to do the most basic parts of my job. So yeah, focusing has been a real uphill battle.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with your team members or friends since the incident with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, my relationships... I mean, I'm still seeing people. I haven't locked myself in my apartment or anything like that. I still go out. Like, I still go to my beach volleyball league on Thursday nights. We play down at the courts near the pier, and usually, it’s the highlight of my week, you know? The sun going down, the sand, just messing around with the guys and grabbing a drink after. And I even had a couple of friends over for dinner this past weekend. I made my famous chili, which is usually a whole production—I slow-cook it all day, get all the toppings laid out, the whole nine yards. \n\nBut... I don't know. It’s hard to explain. Even though I’m still doing all those things, still showing up and talking to my friends and my team at work, it just feels... different. Like I’m just going through the motions. When I was at volleyball, I was serving, diving, doing all the physical stuff, but I wasn't really *there*. I wasn't feeling that rush or that camaraderie I usually get. And with the dinner party, I was sitting there at my own table, watching my friends laugh and eat the food I made, and I just felt completely flat. \n\nIt’s like... normally, those things make me feel good. They’re fun. They’re satisfying. But right now, none of it is landing. I can smile and nod and participate, but inside, I just feel kind of empty or disconnected from the actual joy of it. It’s like the volume on all my good feelings has been turned all the way down to zero. So yeah, I’m still seeing everyone, but it definitely doesn't feel the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, that kind of ties into what I was just saying, honestly. It’s... yeah, it’s exactly that. I mean, I brought up the beach volleyball league, right? I’ve been playing in that league for, gosh, three years now? Ever since I moved into my current place. We play down by the pier, and normally, man, I live for Thursday nights. It’s the perfect way to blow off steam after dealing with the sales floor all week. You get out there, the sand is still a little warm, you get a good sweat going, and you just laugh with the guys. But last Thursday, I went, and I was out there on the court, serving, setting, doing everything I normally do, but I just... I didn't care. I didn't feel that competitive spark, I didn't feel the rush when we scored a point. It was just sand and a net and me moving my arms. \n\nAnd it was the same thing with that dinner party I mentioned. I love cooking. I really do. It’s usually a big creative outlet for me. I spent all Saturday morning at the farmers market picking out the right peppers for the chili, getting the good cuts of meat, simmering it for like six hours. Normally, the smell fills the whole apartment, and I get so excited for people to come over and try it. But this time? The guys came over, they were eating, they were telling jokes, complimenting the food, and I was just sitting at the head of the table feeling... nothing. Just completely blank. \n\nI’m doing the things I used to love, the things that usually recharge my batteries, but it’s like my battery won't take the charge anymore. I’m just going through the motions. I know I’m supposed to be having fun, my brain knows this is a fun activity, but the actual feeling of it being fun or satisfying? It’s just not there. It’s like I’m watching myself do these things from behind a pane of glass. So, yeah, I guess I haven't stopped doing the activities, but the pleasure part of them? That’s completely gone right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about how you handled the situation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Ashamed? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't feel like I did anything wrong, really. If anything, I feel like I handled it the best way I could have, given the circumstances. You have to understand, the regional boss—he's not the kind of guy who listens to reason. He comes in, he looks at a spreadsheet, and he starts making demands without actually understanding the day-to-day operations on the floor. He doesn't know my team. He doesn't know the specific challenges we're facing with our current lead generation software, which, by the way, has been glitching for the last three weeks, making it almost impossible to hit the quotas he was yelling about. \n\nSo when he came at me like that, in front of everyone, I had to stand my ground. I'm a supervisor. My guys need to see that I have their backs. If I just rolled over and let him scream at me about numbers that aren't even accurate, what does that say to my team? It says I won't fight for them. So, no, I don't feel guilty about pushing back. I don't feel ashamed of how I reacted in the moment. I just... I hate that it happened, you know? I hate the whole situation. I hate that it escalated the way it did, and I hate how it's making me feel now—the looping thoughts, the spacing out at my desk, the feeling empty at volleyball. But as for the actual confrontation itself? The way I handled my end of it? No, I don't feel guilty about that. I was just doing my job, trying to protect my guys from a guy who doesn't get it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to your regional boss or interacting with him in any way since the confrontation?\n\nPatient: Avoid him? No, I mean, I haven't been doing that. Given my role as a supervisor, avoiding him isn't really an option anyway, right? I oversee a team of twelve guys on the outbound sales floor. We handle the mid-west territory, mostly B2B software accounts, and the reporting structure is pretty rigid. Every Tuesday and Thursday at 9 AM sharp, I have to send up the lead generation metrics and the conversion rates for the previous forty-eight hours directly to him. I can't just not send those or pretend he doesn't exist, you know? \n\nPlus, we have the weekly regional sync on Monday afternoons. It's a video call, usually about forty-five minutes to an hour, where all the supervisors in the district have to report out on their floor's performance. I still log in. I still give my updates. I don't turn my camera off or suddenly call in sick just because he's running the meeting. I'm there. I mean, sure, I'm not going out of my way to call him up for a friendly chat about the weekend or anything, but I never really did that anyway. We were never buddies, it's always been strictly business. \n\nBut as far as the actual work goes? No, I'm not dodging his emails or ignoring his calls. If he messages me on the company chat or sends an email about the Q3 projections, I respond right away. I'm a professional. I've got a job to do, and I've got my team to look out for, exactly like I was saying before. If avoiding him means I'm neglecting my duties and leaving my guys hanging out to dry with upper management, then absolutely not. I face it head-on. Even if I don't exactly love seeing his name pop up in my inbox right now, I still click it, I still read it, and I still send the data he asks for. So, no, I wouldn't say there's any avoidance happening. I'm just keeping my head down and doing the job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You can't really avoid any part of it without just... not going to work. And I have to go to work. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm still doing all my normal tasks, still walking the same routes to the breakroom or the printer, still sitting in the exact spot where he cornered me. I guess I just kind of power through it? Even though my brain is constantly replaying the whole thing, like I said before, I haven't actually changed my routine or started avoiding anything. I just sit there and deal with the thoughts while I'm trying to get the job done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is a whole other thing, I guess. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping, not in the sense that I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours on end, or tossing and turning all night. I know some guys, when they get stressed out, they just can't shut their eyes, or they wake up at 3:00 AM and can't get back to sleep. But for me, it hasn't really been like that. \n\nI still go to bed around the same time, usually right after I finish watching whatever show I have on, and I seem to fall asleep okay. I mean, my brain is still doing that thing I told you about, replaying the whole argument and the way he looked at me, but eventually, I do drift off. And I usually sleep through the night. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat or having nightmares about him or anything. \n\nSo, I guess the short answer is no, I haven't really noticed any big changes in my sleep. I'm still getting my usual six or seven hours, and I wake up when my alarm goes off. It's just the waking hours that are the problem, you know? The minute I open my eyes, the loop starts up again, but the actual sleeping part seems to be holding up fine. I've always been a pretty heavy sleeper, honestly, even back in college when things got crazy with exams and stuff, I could always just pass out. So yeah, I don't think my sleep has been messed up by all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. That's... that's probably the biggest thing I've noticed, to be honest. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said before, I have a whole team of guys relying on me to keep things moving. If I drop the ball, the whole floor feels it. \n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk—that same glass one I mentioned—and I'll open up Salesforce. We use it for everything, tracking all our client interactions, so normally I’m just zipping right through it, checking my reps' notes, making sure they’re hitting their daily targets and following up on leads. It's second nature to me. But lately? Since that whole blow-up? I keep opening Salesforce to check those notes, and I’ll just... stall out. I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same account on my monitor for ten minutes. Just staring at it. Not actually reading a single word of what my guy typed in, just completely blanking out while my brain is off doing that looping thing I told you about. \n\nAnd it's not just the computer stuff, either. It’s the day-to-day decision-making. As a supervisor, I have to make these rapid-fire calls all day long, and I've always been good at it. But now, even simple calls, like figuring out who should cover a client visit if someone calls out sick, they just feel so much harder to decide than they should. Normally, I’d just look at the schedule, see who’s in the right territory, and boom, it's handled. But now I sit there weighing all these stupid little variables, second-guessing myself, overthinking the logistics until I realize I've wasted twenty minutes on a choice that should take thirty seconds. It’s like my brain is just wading through molasses trying to do the most basic parts of my job. So yeah, focusing has been a real uphill battle.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with your team members or friends since the incident with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, my relationships... I mean, I'm still seeing people. I haven't locked myself in my apartment or anything like that. I still go out. Like, I still go to my beach volleyball league on Thursday nights. We play down at the courts near the pier, and usually, it’s the highlight of my week, you know? The sun going down, the sand, just messing around with the guys and grabbing a drink after. And I even had a couple of friends over for dinner this past weekend. I made my famous chili, which is usually a whole production—I slow-cook it all day, get all the toppings laid out, the whole nine yards. \n\nBut... I don't know. It’s hard to explain. Even though I’m still doing all those things, still showing up and talking to my friends and my team at work, it just feels... different. Like I’m just going through the motions. When I was at volleyball, I was serving, diving, doing all the physical stuff, but I wasn't really *there*. I wasn't feeling that rush or that camaraderie I usually get. And with the dinner party, I was sitting there at my own table, watching my friends laugh and eat the food I made, and I just felt completely flat. \n\nIt’s like... normally, those things make me feel good. They’re fun. They’re satisfying. But right now, none of it is landing. I can smile and nod and participate, but inside, I just feel kind of empty or disconnected from the actual joy of it. It’s like the volume on all my good feelings has been turned all the way down to zero. So yeah, I’m still seeing everyone, but it definitely doesn't feel the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, that kind of ties into what I was just saying, honestly. It’s... yeah, it’s exactly that. I mean, I brought up the beach volleyball league, right? I’ve been playing in that league for, gosh, three years now? Ever since I moved into my current place. We play down by the pier, and normally, man, I live for Thursday nights. It’s the perfect way to blow off steam after dealing with the sales floor all week. You get out there, the sand is still a little warm, you get a good sweat going, and you just laugh with the guys. But last Thursday, I went, and I was out there on the court, serving, setting, doing everything I normally do, but I just... I didn't care. I didn't feel that competitive spark, I didn't feel the rush when we scored a point. It was just sand and a net and me moving my arms. \n\nAnd it was the same thing with that dinner party I mentioned. I love cooking. I really do. It’s usually a big creative outlet for me. I spent all Saturday morning at the farmers market picking out the right peppers for the chili, getting the good cuts of meat, simmering it for like six hours. Normally, the smell fills the whole apartment, and I get so excited for people to come over and try it. But this time? The guys came over, they were eating, they were telling jokes, complimenting the food, and I was just sitting at the head of the table feeling... nothing. Just completely blank. \n\nI’m doing the things I used to love, the things that usually recharge my batteries, but it’s like my battery won't take the charge anymore. I’m just going through the motions. I know I’m supposed to be having fun, my brain knows this is a fun activity, but the actual feeling of it being fun or satisfying? It’s just not there. It’s like I’m watching myself do these things from behind a pane of glass. So, yeah, I guess I haven't stopped doing the activities, but the pleasure part of them? That’s completely gone right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about how you handled the situation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Ashamed? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't feel like I did anything wrong, really. If anything, I feel like I handled it the best way I could have, given the circumstances. You have to understand, the regional boss—he's not the kind of guy who listens to reason. He comes in, he looks at a spreadsheet, and he starts making demands without actually understanding the day-to-day operations on the floor. He doesn't know my team. He doesn't know the specific challenges we're facing with our current lead generation software, which, by the way, has been glitching for the last three weeks, making it almost impossible to hit the quotas he was yelling about. \n\nSo when he came at me like that, in front of everyone, I had to stand my ground. I'm a supervisor. My guys need to see that I have their backs. If I just rolled over and let him scream at me about numbers that aren't even accurate, what does that say to my team? It says I won't fight for them. So, no, I don't feel guilty about pushing back. I don't feel ashamed of how I reacted in the moment. I just... I hate that it happened, you know? I hate the whole situation. I hate that it escalated the way it did, and I hate how it's making me feel now—the looping thoughts, the spacing out at my desk, the feeling empty at volleyball. But as for the actual confrontation itself? The way I handled my end of it? No, I don't feel guilty about that. I was just doing my job, trying to protect my guys from a guy who doesn't get it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to your regional boss or interacting with him in any way since the confrontation?\n\nPatient: Avoid him? No, I mean, I haven't been doing that. Given my role as a supervisor, avoiding him isn't really an option anyway, right? I oversee a team of twelve guys on the outbound sales floor. We handle the mid-west territory, mostly B2B software accounts, and the reporting structure is pretty rigid. Every Tuesday and Thursday at 9 AM sharp, I have to send up the lead generation metrics and the conversion rates for the previous forty-eight hours directly to him. I can't just not send those or pretend he doesn't exist, you know? \n\nPlus, we have the weekly regional sync on Monday afternoons. It's a video call, usually about forty-five minutes to an hour, where all the supervisors in the district have to report out on their floor's performance. I still log in. I still give my updates. I don't turn my camera off or suddenly call in sick just because he's running the meeting. I'm there. I mean, sure, I'm not going out of my way to call him up for a friendly chat about the weekend or anything, but I never really did that anyway. We were never buddies, it's always been strictly business. \n\nBut as far as the actual work goes? No, I'm not dodging his emails or ignoring his calls. If he messages me on the company chat or sends an email about the Q3 projections, I respond right away. I'm a professional. I've got a job to do, and I've got my team to look out for, exactly like I was saying before. If avoiding him means I'm neglecting my duties and leaving my guys hanging out to dry with upper management, then absolutely not. I face it head-on. Even if I don't exactly love seeing his name pop up in my inbox right now, I still click it, I still read it, and I still send the data he asks for. So, no, I wouldn't say there's any avoidance happening. I'm just keeping my head down and doing the job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I didn't feel the joy of it, you know, that blank feeling I was talking about, but I still ate. I still had a bowl. 

And my regular routine hasn't really changed either. I usually grab a bagel and a coffee from the cart downstairs before I head up to the sales floor—everything bagel, toasted, light cream cheese, large black coffee. I've been doing that every morning since I started this job, and I'm still doing it. I eat it at my desk while I'm reviewing the overnight lead reports before the guys get in. And for lunch, I usually just bring leftovers or grab a salad from the deli across the street if I'm pressed for time, which, honestly, with the software glitching lately, I've been doing more often than not. Just a basic grilled chicken salad, maybe some balsamic dressing. 

So, yeah, I'm still eating my normal meals. I'm not skipping anything, and I'm not, like, stress-eating an entire pizza at midnight either. My weight hasn't fluctuated or anything like that. I'm still hungry when it gets to be around noon, and I still want dinner when I get home after volleyball or whatever. It's just... the food is just fuel right now, I guess. I'm eating because I know I need to eat to keep my energy up, especially with how demanding the floor is right now and having to keep the team focused despite all the noise from regional. But the actual appetite itself? The physical feeling of being hungry and eating? That's all completely normal. Nothing's really changed on that front.

I was totally alone on the floor with him, no security around, no other managers, and I just remember feeling completely trapped in that little glass office while he lost his mind at me. \n\nSo... yeah. That was two weeks ago. And ever since that specific night, I just can't shake it. I walk into the building and my brain just goes right back to that moment, just waiting for him or someone else to appear out of nowhere and start slamming things again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that confrontation with your regional boss, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly like it's happening all over again. I mean, I don't feel like I'm actually back in that room with him screaming at me, if that's what you mean. It's not like a hallucination or anything like that. I know I'm sitting at my desk in the present day. \n\nBut I do think about it a lot. Like, constantly. It just replays in my head, over and over, especially when I'm trying to concentrate on something else. Like, I'll be trying to draft an email to the team about their quarterly goals, and suddenly my brain is just replaying the exact way he slammed his hand down on the glass desk, or the exact tone of his voice when he was yelling about the lost account. And it's so frustrating because I'm trying to work, I'm trying to do my job, and I just get derailed by these memories. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I'll be at home, trying to watch a movie or make dinner, and out of nowhere, I'm just thinking about how dark the office was, how quiet it was before he showed up, and how trapped I felt. It's like my brain just won't let it go. I'll spend twenty minutes just stewing over it, thinking about what I should have said, or how I should have handled it differently, or just feeling that same sense of panic I felt when he was cornering me. But no, I don't feel like I'm literally reliving it. It's just... it's just always there, playing on a loop in the background of my mind, you know? It's like I can't escape the memory of it, even when I'm miles away from the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any physical sensations, like your heart racing or sweating, when you think about the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I mean, I know people talk about that, right? Like, breaking out in a cold sweat or feeling like they're having a heart attack when they think about something stressful. But for me, it's not like that. When the memory pops into my head—which, like I said, is all the time, whether I'm trying to write an email or just sitting on my couch at home—my heart doesn't start pounding out of my chest. I don't get all clammy or sweaty. \n\nIt's more just... mental, I guess? Like, my brain gets completely hijacked by the memory of him slamming his hands on the desk, and I just get stuck in this loop of frustration and anger and feeling trapped all over again. But my body itself? No, it doesn't really react. My breathing stays normal, my pulse doesn't go crazy. I just sit there, perfectly still, while my mind just races through the whole thing for the hundredth time. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweating or anything physical like that when I'm just thinking about it. It's just the thoughts that won't stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain areas of the office or specific tasks at work because they remind you of the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually been thinking a lot about how I navigate the office lately. But... no, not really. I mean, I haven't been avoiding any specific areas or tasks. \n\nI know it might make sense to, say, avoid my own office where it happened, or stay away from the glass desks, or maybe even try to get out of doing the end-of-month spreadsheet cleanup since that's what I was doing when he walked in. But honestly, I can't really do that. I'm a supervisor, you know? I have a team of guys who rely on me to get those metrics sorted out, and if I don't do it, it just doesn't get done. The whole branch's numbers would be off. So I still sit at that exact same glass desk, in that exact same office, and I still stay late to do the spreadsheets when I have to. \n\nAnd as for the rest of the building, it's not a huge office anyway. There's the main floor where the guys have their cubicles, a little breakroom with a terrible coffee machine, a couple of conference rooms, and then the glass offices for management along the back wall. You can't really avoid any part of it without just... not going to work. And I have to go to work. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm still doing all my normal tasks, still walking the same routes to the breakroom or the printer, still sitting in the exact spot where he cornered me. I guess I just kind of power through it? Even though my brain is constantly replaying the whole thing, like I said before, I haven't actually changed my routine or started avoiding anything. I just sit there and deal with the thoughts while I'm trying to get the job done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is a whole other thing, I guess. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping, not in the sense that I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours on end, or tossing and turning all night. I know some guys, when they get stressed out, they just can't shut their eyes, or they wake up at 3:00 AM and can't get back to sleep. But for me, it hasn't really been like that. \n\nI still go to bed around the same time, usually right after I finish watching whatever show I have on, and I seem to fall asleep okay. I mean, my brain is still doing that thing I told you about, replaying the whole argument and the way he looked at me, but eventually, I do drift off. And I usually sleep through the night. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat or having nightmares about him or anything. \n\nSo, I guess the short answer is no, I haven't really noticed any big changes in my sleep. I'm still getting my usual six or seven hours, and I wake up when my alarm goes off. It's just the waking hours that are the problem, you know? The minute I open my eyes, the loop starts up again, but the actual sleeping part seems to be holding up fine. I've always been a pretty heavy sleeper, honestly, even back in college when things got crazy with exams and stuff, I could always just pass out. So yeah, I don't think my sleep has been messed up by all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks or activities since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. That's... that's probably the biggest thing I've noticed, to be honest. It’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said before, I have a whole team of guys relying on me to keep things moving. If I drop the ball, the whole floor feels it. \n\nI’ll be sitting at my desk—that same glass one I mentioned—and I'll open up Salesforce. We use it for everything, tracking all our client interactions, so normally I’m just zipping right through it, checking my reps' notes, making sure they’re hitting their daily targets and following up on leads. It's second nature to me. But lately? Since that whole blow-up? I keep opening Salesforce to check those notes, and I’ll just... stall out. I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same account on my monitor for ten minutes. Just staring at it. Not actually reading a single word of what my guy typed in, just completely blanking out while my brain is off doing that looping thing I told you about. \n\nAnd it's not just the computer stuff, either. It’s the day-to-day decision-making. As a supervisor, I have to make these rapid-fire calls all day long, and I've always been good at it. But now, even simple calls, like figuring out who should cover a client visit if someone calls out sick, they just feel so much harder to decide than they should. Normally, I’d just look at the schedule, see who’s in the right territory, and boom, it's handled. But now I sit there weighing all these stupid little variables, second-guessing myself, overthinking the logistics until I realize I've wasted twenty minutes on a choice that should take thirty seconds. It’s like my brain is just wading through molasses trying to do the most basic parts of my job. So yeah, focusing has been a real uphill battle.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships with your team members or friends since the incident with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, my relationships... I mean, I'm still seeing people. I haven't locked myself in my apartment or anything like that. I still go out. Like, I still go to my beach volleyball league on Thursday nights. We play down at the courts near the pier, and usually, it’s the highlight of my week, you know? The sun going down, the sand, just messing around with the guys and grabbing a drink after. And I even had a couple of friends over for dinner this past weekend. I made my famous chili, which is usually a whole production—I slow-cook it all day, get all the toppings laid out, the whole nine yards. \n\nBut... I don't know. It’s hard to explain. Even though I’m still doing all those things, still showing up and talking to my friends and my team at work, it just feels... different. Like I’m just going through the motions. When I was at volleyball, I was serving, diving, doing all the physical stuff, but I wasn't really *there*. I wasn't feeling that rush or that camaraderie I usually get. And with the dinner party, I was sitting there at my own table, watching my friends laugh and eat the food I made, and I just felt completely flat. \n\nIt’s like... normally, those things make me feel good. They’re fun. They’re satisfying. But right now, none of it is landing. I can smile and nod and participate, but inside, I just feel kind of empty or disconnected from the actual joy of it. It’s like the volume on all my good feelings has been turned all the way down to zero. So yeah, I’m still seeing everyone, but it definitely doesn't feel the same to me.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, that kind of ties into what I was just saying, honestly. It’s... yeah, it’s exactly that. I mean, I brought up the beach volleyball league, right? I’ve been playing in that league for, gosh, three years now? Ever since I moved into my current place. We play down by the pier, and normally, man, I live for Thursday nights. It’s the perfect way to blow off steam after dealing with the sales floor all week. You get out there, the sand is still a little warm, you get a good sweat going, and you just laugh with the guys. But last Thursday, I went, and I was out there on the court, serving, setting, doing everything I normally do, but I just... I didn't care. I didn't feel that competitive spark, I didn't feel the rush when we scored a point. It was just sand and a net and me moving my arms. \n\nAnd it was the same thing with that dinner party I mentioned. I love cooking. I really do. It’s usually a big creative outlet for me. I spent all Saturday morning at the farmers market picking out the right peppers for the chili, getting the good cuts of meat, simmering it for like six hours. Normally, the smell fills the whole apartment, and I get so excited for people to come over and try it. But this time? The guys came over, they were eating, they were telling jokes, complimenting the food, and I was just sitting at the head of the table feeling... nothing. Just completely blank. \n\nI’m doing the things I used to love, the things that usually recharge my batteries, but it’s like my battery won't take the charge anymore. I’m just going through the motions. I know I’m supposed to be having fun, my brain knows this is a fun activity, but the actual feeling of it being fun or satisfying? It’s just not there. It’s like I’m watching myself do these things from behind a pane of glass. So, yeah, I guess I haven't stopped doing the activities, but the pleasure part of them? That’s completely gone right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed about how you handled the situation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Ashamed? No, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't feel like I did anything wrong, really. If anything, I feel like I handled it the best way I could have, given the circumstances. You have to understand, the regional boss—he's not the kind of guy who listens to reason. He comes in, he looks at a spreadsheet, and he starts making demands without actually understanding the day-to-day operations on the floor. He doesn't know my team. He doesn't know the specific challenges we're facing with our current lead generation software, which, by the way, has been glitching for the last three weeks, making it almost impossible to hit the quotas he was yelling about. \n\nSo when he came at me like that, in front of everyone, I had to stand my ground. I'm a supervisor. My guys need to see that I have their backs. If I just rolled over and let him scream at me about numbers that aren't even accurate, what does that say to my team? It says I won't fight for them. So, no, I don't feel guilty about pushing back. I don't feel ashamed of how I reacted in the moment. I just... I hate that it happened, you know? I hate the whole situation. I hate that it escalated the way it did, and I hate how it's making me feel now—the looping thoughts, the spacing out at my desk, the feeling empty at volleyball. But as for the actual confrontation itself? The way I handled my end of it? No, I don't feel guilty about that. I was just doing my job, trying to protect my guys from a guy who doesn't get it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding talking to your regional boss or interacting with him in any way since the confrontation?\n\nPatient: Avoid him? No, I mean, I haven't been doing that. Given my role as a supervisor, avoiding him isn't really an option anyway, right? I oversee a team of twelve guys on the outbound sales floor. We handle the mid-west territory, mostly B2B software accounts, and the reporting structure is pretty rigid. Every Tuesday and Thursday at 9 AM sharp, I have to send up the lead generation metrics and the conversion rates for the previous forty-eight hours directly to him. I can't just not send those or pretend he doesn't exist, you know? \n\nPlus, we have the weekly regional sync on Monday afternoons. It's a video call, usually about forty-five minutes to an hour, where all the supervisors in the district have to report out on their floor's performance. I still log in. I still give my updates. I don't turn my camera off or suddenly call in sick just because he's running the meeting. I'm there. I mean, sure, I'm not going out of my way to call him up for a friendly chat about the weekend or anything, but I never really did that anyway. We were never buddies, it's always been strictly business. \n\nBut as far as the actual work goes? No, I'm not dodging his emails or ignoring his calls. If he messages me on the company chat or sends an email about the Q3 projections, I respond right away. I'm a professional. I've got a job to do, and I've got my team to look out for, exactly like I was saying before. If avoiding him means I'm neglecting my duties and leaving my guys hanging out to dry with upper management, then absolutely not. I face it head-on. Even if I don't exactly love seeing his name pop up in my inbox right now, I still click it, I still read it, and I still send the data he asks for. So, no, I wouldn't say there's any avoidance happening. I'm just keeping my head down and doing the job.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite since the incident with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Um, no, not really. I mean, I haven't noticed any major changes there. Like I was saying with that dinner party, I still cooked the chili, I still went to the farmers market and picked everything out, and I still sat down and ate it with the guys. I didn't feel the joy of it, you know, that blank feeling I was talking about, but I still ate. I still had a bowl. \n\nAnd my regular routine hasn't really changed either. I usually grab a bagel and a coffee from the cart downstairs before I head up to the sales floor—everything bagel, toasted, light cream cheese, large black coffee. I've been doing that every morning since I started this job, and I'm still doing it. I eat it at my desk while I'm reviewing the overnight lead reports before the guys get in. And for lunch, I usually just bring leftovers or grab a salad from the deli across the street if I'm pressed for time, which, honestly, with the software glitching lately, I've been doing more often than not. Just a basic grilled chicken salad, maybe some balsamic dressing. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm still eating my normal meals. I'm not skipping anything, and I'm not, like, stress-eating an entire pizza at midnight either. My weight hasn't fluctuated or anything like that. I'm still hungry when it gets to be around noon, and I still want dinner when I get home after volleyball or whatever. It's just... the food is just fuel right now, I guess. I'm eating because I know I need to eat to keep my energy up, especially with how demanding the floor is right now and having to keep the team focused despite all the noise from regional. But the actual appetite itself? The physical feeling of being hungry and eating? That's all completely normal. Nothing's really changed on that front.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Threats? Dangers? Um... well, I wouldn't say I'm looking out for, like, physical danger, you know? It's an office building. We've got security downstairs, keycard access, the whole nine yards. But... I guess, in a way, yeah, I am on high alert. Especially when I'm actually in the office, on the floor with the guys.

Like, my desk is right in the middle of the bullpen, so I can see all twelve of my guys, which is great for supervising, but it also means my back is to the main walkway that leads from the elevators. And lately, I just keep... I keep checking over my shoulder. Every time I hear the elevator ding, or footsteps coming down the hall, I'm turning around to see who it is. I'm constantly scanning the room to see where my boss is, or if he's suddenly going to appear out of nowhere again like he did that day. 

And it's not just him. I find myself looking at the exits, too. Like, calculating how fast I could get out of the room if things went south, or if he started yelling again. Which is crazy, right? It's a sales floor, not a war zone. But I'll be sitting there, supposed to be listening in on a call one of my guys is making, trying to coach them on closing a tough B2B software account, and instead of focusing on their pitch, I'm staring at the glass doors at the end of the hall. I'm tracking every person who walks by. I'm practically jumping out of my skin if someone drops a stapler or a chair squeaks too loud. 

I just... I feel like I have to know where everyone is at all times, especially him, and especially who's behind me. It's exhausting, honestly. I'm trying to do my job, trying to be there for my team like I said, but half my brain is just stuck on radar duty, waiting for the next attack, even when there's literally no reason to think anything's going to happen.

Well, like I said before, I’m still making it to work. I haven't missed a single shift, I'm still swiping my badge at 8:15 every morning. But... man, it’s definitely getting harder to keep all the plates spinning. I’m just... I’m so much slower right now. Usually, when I’m looking over the B2B software accounts or reviewing the daily lead sheets, I can just breeze through the metrics, spot any discrepancies, and fire off my adjustments in five or ten minutes. But lately? I’ll be staring at the spreadsheets and the numbers just kind of blur together. It takes me twice as long to process a simple account update because I keep losing my train of thought. 

And it's not just the accounts, it's the staffing decisions, too. I've got twelve guys on the floor, right? So scheduling their call blocks, figuring out who’s covering the inbound lines during the lunch rush, managing their PTO requests... usually, I do that in my sleep. It's second nature. But now I’m second-guessing everything. I’ll make a schedule and then just sit there staring at it, wondering if I completely messed up the coverage. I’ve actually had to start asking a couple of my senior guys on the team to double-check things for me. Like, I'll pull them aside and say, "Hey, can you just look this over and make sure I didn't double-book anyone?" Which is honestly pretty embarrassing. I’m the supervisor. I shouldn’t be asking my reps to make sure I didn't mess up the lead distribution. I’ve always handled all of that entirely on my own, no problem. 

And the timing of all this just couldn't be worse. We’ve got the quarterly performance reviews coming up in a few weeks, and that’s a huge deal in our district. It dictates bonuses, territory assignments, everything. And the regional boss—the one who blew up at me—he personally reviews all my evaluations of the team, plus my own supervisor metrics. I’m just... I’m really worried that these little mistakes, this slowness, it’s going to show up on the reports and he's going to use it against me. I'm terrified these slip-ups are going to seriously affect my job. I just can’t seem to get my brain to fire on all cylinders right now, and with those reviews looming over my head, it’s just making me panic that I'm going to ruin my career, or let my guys down, just because I can't think straight at my desk.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I, uh... I guess I'm here because I called the EAP—the Employee Assistance Program at my company. I supervise a team of sales reps, you know, the guys who handle corporate accounts, not retail, so it's a lot of phones, a lot of back-and-forth, a lot of moving parts. It's usually a pretty lively floor. But lately, I just... I can't handle the noise. It's not even loud noise, it's just... sudden things. \n\nWhat actually made me pick up the phone and call the EAP is that every little bang in the office is just destroying my nerves. Like, yesterday, one of my reps just slammed a file drawer shut—not even mad, just, you know, in a hurry—and I practically jumped out of my skin. I spilled coffee on my desk. And it's not just the jump, it's that my heart just starts pounding, like, slamming against my ribs, and my chest keeps racing way, way longer than it should. It takes me like ten minutes to calm down from someone just dropping a coffee mug or a heavy binder hitting a desk. I mean, I'm 27, I shouldn't be jumping at shadows like some kind of... I don't know. It's just exhausting, and I can't keep working like this with my heart going crazy every time someone shuts a door too fast.\n\nDoctor: Are these strong reactions to noise happening only at work or are you experiencing them in other settings as well?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't think so. I mean, I haven't really noticed it happening anywhere else, but, to be fair, my apartment is pretty quiet. I live by myself, so there's no one around to, you know, slam a door or drop a plate or anything like that. It's just me and the TV. And I guess when I'm driving to work, the noise is more... predictable? Like, you expect cars to be loud, you expect the highway to rumble, so it's not catching me off guard. \n\nIt's really just the office where I'm constantly on edge. I mean, maybe if a car backfired right next to me on the street I'd lose my mind, but that hasn't happened. So, yeah, I guess I haven't really had those heart-pounding moments at home or at the grocery store or anything. But at work, it's just so exhausting. Like I was saying, it's a sales floor, so you've got people pacing while they're on headsets, you've got chairs rolling around on those plastic mats, people tossing heavy staplers down on their desks... and I'm just sitting there in my office with the glass walls, practically holding my breath, just waiting for the next sudden crack or bang. So, no, I haven't really noticed it outside of the office. Just... just at work, mostly. I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge at work even when it's quiet?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when it's totally quiet, which honestly is rare, but even then... I'm just constantly scanning the room. Like, I sit in this office with glass walls, right? It's supposed to make me look accessible to the team, but lately, it just makes me feel exposed. I catch myself constantly checking where my boss is, you know? Like, is he walking down the hall? Is he about to come in? And then I'm looking at the exits, figuring out the quickest way out of the room if I needed to leave right that second, which makes zero sense because it's just an office building, but I can't stop doing it. \n\nAnd if someone walks behind my chair—even if they're just walking past to get to the printer—my neck just gets so stiff. I'm always checking to see if someone is coming up behind me. I'm supposed to be looking at sales metrics and listening in on calls to coach my reps, but instead, I'm just watching the reflection in my computer screen to see who's moving behind me. It's like I can't turn off this radar in my head. I'm just sitting there, waiting for something to happen, even when absolutely nothing is going on. It's just exhausting to be on guard like that all day long.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your job or work environment that you think might be causing you to feel this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, there is. I guess I probably should have started with that, huh? It didn't just come out of nowhere. This whole thing, all the jumping and the heart pounding and constantly checking over my shoulder... it's been going on for about two weeks now. And it started exactly after this... this really intense, late-night confrontation I had with my regional boss. \n\nIt was a Tuesday, I think, and I was working late—like, really late, maybe 9:30 or 10:00 PM. The end of the month is always a huge mess because half the guys never log their call metrics properly in the system, so I was the only one left on the floor trying to clean up the spreadsheets. The overhead lights were actually on that motion-sensor timer, so half the office was completely pitch black. Anyway, my regional boss, who normally works out of the corporate hub downtown and is almost never at our branch anyway, just... appeared. I didn't hear the elevator chime, I didn't hear the main double doors open. He just suddenly was right there in the doorway of my office, and he was absolutely livid about a massive corporate account that had fallen through earlier that day. \n\nHe came in, totally cornered me, and just started yelling. He was getting right up in my physical space, leaning over my desk, slamming his hands down on the glass... it was just so aggressive and completely out of left field. I was totally alone on the floor with him, no security around, no other managers, and I just remember feeling completely trapped in that little glass office while he lost his mind at me. \n\nSo... yeah. That was two weeks ago. And ever since that specific night, I just can't shake it. I walk into the building and my brain just goes right back to that moment, just waiting for him or someone else to appear out of nowhere and start slamming things again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving that confrontation with your regional boss, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly like it's happening all over again. I mean, I don't feel like I'm actually back in that room with him screaming at me, if that's what you mean. It's not like a hallucination or anything like that. I know I'm sitting at my desk in the present day. \n\nBut I do think about it a lot. Like, constantly. It just replays in my head, over and over, especially when I'm trying to concentrate on something else. Like, I'll be trying to draft an email to the team about their quarterly goals, and suddenly my brain is just replaying the exact way he slammed his hand down on the glass desk, or the exact tone of his voice when he was yelling about the lost account. And it's so frustrating because I'm trying to work, I'm trying to do my job, and I just get derailed by these memories. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I'll be at home, trying to watch a movie or make dinner, and out of nowhere, I'm just thinking about how dark the office was, how quiet it was before he showed up, and how trapped I felt. It's like my brain just won't let it go. I'll spend twenty minutes just stewing over it, thinking about what I should have said, or how I should have handled it differently, or just feeling that same sense of panic I felt when he was cornering me. But no, I don't feel like I'm literally reliving it. It's just... it's just always there, playing on a loop in the background of my mind, you know? It's like I can't escape the memory of it, even when I'm miles away from the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel any physical sensations, like your heart racing or sweating, when you think about the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Honestly... no, not really. I mean, I know people talk about that, right? Like, breaking out in a cold sweat or feeling like they're having a heart attack when they think about something stressful. But for me, it's not like that. When the memory pops into my head—which, like I said, is all the time, whether I'm trying to write an email or just sitting on my couch at home—my heart doesn't start pounding out of my chest. I don't get all clammy or sweaty. \n\nIt's more just... mental, I guess? Like, my brain gets completely hijacked by the memory of him slamming his hands on the desk, and I just get stuck in this loop of frustration and anger and feeling trapped all over again. But my body itself? No, it doesn't really react. My breathing stays normal, my pulse doesn't go crazy. I just sit there, perfectly still, while my mind just races through the whole thing for the hundredth time. So, yeah, no racing heart or sweating or anything physical like that when I'm just thinking about it. It's just the thoughts that won't stop.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding certain areas of the office or specific tasks at work because they remind you of the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually been thinking a lot about how I navigate the office lately. But... no, not really. I mean, I haven't been avoiding any specific areas or tasks. \n\nI know it might make sense to, say, avoid my own office where it happened, or stay away from the glass desks, or maybe even try to get out of doing the end-of-month spreadsheet cleanup since that's what I was doing when he walked in. But honestly, I can't really do that. I'm a supervisor, you know? I have a team of guys who rely on me to get those metrics sorted out, and if I don't do it, it just doesn't get done. The whole branch's numbers would be off. So I still sit at that exact same glass desk, in that exact same office, and I still stay late to do the spreadsheets when I have to. \n\nAnd as for the rest of the building, it's not a huge office anyway. There's the main floor where the guys have their cubicles, a little breakroom with a terrible coffee machine, a couple of conference rooms, and then the glass offices for management along the back wall. You can't really avoid any part of it without just... not going to work. And I have to go to work. \n\nSo, yeah, I'm still doing all my normal tasks, still walking the same routes to the breakroom or the printer, still sitting in the exact spot where he cornered me. I guess I just kind of power through it? Even though my brain is constantly replaying the whole thing, like I said before, I haven't actually changed my routine or started avoiding anything. I just sit there and deal with the thoughts while I'm trying to get the job done.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the confrontation with your regional boss?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is a whole other thing, I guess. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping, not in the sense that I'm lying awake staring at the ceiling for hours on end, or tossing and turning all night. I know some guys, when they get stressed out, they just can't shut their eyes, or they wake up at 3:00 AM and can't get back to sleep. But for me, it hasn't really been like that. \n\nI still go to bed around the same time, usually right after I finish watching whatever show I have on, and I seem to fall asleep okay.
